    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{table}[t!]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Seven distributional features. ``Entropy'' features are computed as per-token NLL under Pythia-410M (see \\S\\ref{sec:features}); surface features require no reference model. DDR analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) reveals that entropy mean and the two surface features (TTR, hapax ratio) drift with depth while entropy max, min, and tail decay remain near-stationary; entropy std is intermediate. All ``entropy'' features are reference-model NLL values (cross-entropy with Pythia-410M), not information-theoretic entropy; see \\S\\ref{sec:features}.}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{3.8cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Feature} & \\textbf{Source} & \\textbf{Measures} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\texttt{entropy\\_mean} & Ref.\\ NLL & Central tendency of token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_std}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Spread of token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_max}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Maximum token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_min}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Minimum token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{tail\\_decay}   & Ref.\\ NLL & Log-prob decay slope (ranks 100--1K) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\texttt{ttr}           & Surface & Type-token ratio \\\\\n\\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}  & Surface & Proportion of hapax legomena \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Task Formulation}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven a text $x$, we predict its \\emph{generation depth} $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2{+}\\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).\nA generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \\sim \\mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \\geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.\nDepths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}\n\\label{sec:generation}\n\nWe construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \\textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \\cite{pythia}, \\textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \\cite{olmo}, \\textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \\cite{qwen3}, \\textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \\cite{llama3}, and \\textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \\cite{mistral7b}.\nThese generators span five distinct architectures with a 13$\\times$ parameter range (0.6B--8B), trained on heterogeneous corpora; Qwen contributes three models (0.6B--8B) covering the full scale range; Qwen's overrepresentation (3/8 generators) is a limitation, but LOFO holding out all three Qwen models (F1\\,=\\,0.686) provides the strictest family-level test.\n\nFor each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\\mathcal{C}_0$.\nThe same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.\nWe use shared seeds across generators so that cross-generator comparisons control for input content and isolate the effect of the generation model from source text variation.\nIn LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.\nWe address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}).\nEach seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\\texttt{Continue the following text: \\{text\\}}''.\nWe generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class analysis shows adjacent depths 2--4 become indistinguishable (F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.27; Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.\nAll generation uses temperature $\\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.\nAfter filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Extraction}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\n\\paragraph{Terminology.} Throughout this paper, we refer to per-token cross-entropy loss under a fixed reference model as ``entropy'' for conciseness; these are reference-model NLL values, not true information-theoretic entropy of the generating distribution.\n\nWe deliberately use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$11.6\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition.\nLeave-one-feature-out analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}) confirms that both reference-model and surface feature groups contribute independently.\n\nWe extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\\ref{tab:features}), selected to provide principled coverage of the NLL distribution---location (mean), spread (std), extremes (max, min), and tail structure (decay)---complemented by two reference-free surface diversity measures.\nWe evaluated six additional NLL statistics (median, 90th percentile, IQR, CV, skewness, kurtosis) as candidate features; all exhibit $|r| > 0.65$ with existing features and yield no classification gain when added ($\\Delta$F1\\,$\\leq$\\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-candidate}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-model features.}\nA held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nAlthough Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the eight generators, cross-family reference validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}) shows F1 spread of only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic; however, all tested references are causal LMs.\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in ranks 100--1000, capturing the power-law regime where depth-induced compression is most detectable while avoiding high-frequency head noise and sparse extreme-tail counts.\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nTwo features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\\_ratio).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThese features exhibit a \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).\nTTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-validation.}\nWe evaluate generalization via \\textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: each fold holds out one generator entirely, so the classifier must generalize to a generator absent from training---the strictest per-model protocol for assessing deployment against previously unseen generators.\nLOGO does not isolate family-level effects; \\textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\nGenerator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).\n\n\\paragraph{Classifier.}\nWe use XGBoost \\cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$; fixed without tuning), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}.\nAlternative classifiers (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \\cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}); discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($n{=}1{,}000$).\nAll $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n",

          " \\footnotesize",
          " \\caption{Seven distributional features. ``Entropy'' features are computed as per-token NLL under Pythia-410M (see \\S\\ref{sec:features}); surface features require no reference model. DDR analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) reveals that entropy mean and the two surface features (TTR, hapax ratio) drift with depth while entropy max, min, and tail decay remain near-stationary; entropy std is intermediate. All ``entropy'' features are reference-model NLL values (cross-entropy with Pythia-410M), not information-theoretic entropy; see \\S\\ref{sec:features}.}",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{table}[t!]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Seven distributional features. ``Entropy'' features are computed as per-token NLL under Pythia-410M (see \\S\\ref{sec:features}); surface features require no reference model. DDR analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) reveals that entropy mean and the two surface features (TTR, hapax ratio) drift with depth while entropy max, min, and tail decay remain near-stationary; entropy std is intermediate. All ``entropy'' features are reference-model NLL values (cross-entropy with Pythia-410M), not information-theoretic entropy; see \\S\\ref{sec:features}.}\n\\label{tab:features}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llp{3.0cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Feature} & \\textbf{Source} & \\textbf{Measures} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\texttt{entropy\\_mean} & Ref.\\ NLL & Central tendency of token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_std}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Spread of token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_max}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Maximum token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{entropy\\_min}  & Ref.\\ NLL & Minimum token surprise \\\\\n\\texttt{tail\\_decay}   & Ref.\\ NLL & Log-prob decay slope (ranks 100--1K) \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\texttt{ttr}           & Surface & Type-token ratio \\\\\n\\texttt{hapax\\_ratio}  & Surface & Proportion of hapax legomena \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Task Formulation}\n\\label{sec:task}\n\nGiven a text $x$, we predict its \\emph{generation depth} $d \\in \\{0, 1, 2{+}\\}$: human-written ($d{=}0$), first-generation LLM output ($d{=}1$), or text produced by two or more recursive LLM generations ($d{=}2{+}$).\nA generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \\sim \\mathcal{C}_0$ (human corpus), $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \\geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic autoregressive generation.\nDepths beyond 2 are merged into $2{+}$ because feature convergence compresses inter-class margins at deeper levels (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}\n\\label{sec:generation}\n\nWe construct generation chains using eight generators spanning five model families: \\textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \\cite{pythia}, \\textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \\cite{olmo}, \\textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \\cite{qwen3}, \\textbf{Llama} (Llama-3.2-3B) \\cite{llama3}, and \\textbf{Mistral} (Mistral-7B-v0.3) \\cite{mistral7b}.\nThese generators span five distinct architectures with a 13$\\times$ parameter range (0.6B--8B), trained on heterogeneous corpora; Qwen contributes three models (0.6B--8B) covering the full scale range; Qwen's overrepresentation (3/8 generators) is a limitation, but LOFO holding out all three Qwen models (F1\\,=\\,0.686) provides the strictest family-level test.\n\nFor each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\\mathcal{C}_0$.\nThe same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.\nWe use shared seeds across generators so that cross-generator comparisons control for input content and isolate the effect of the generation model from source text variation.\nIn LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.\nWe address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}).\nEach seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\\texttt{Continue the following text: \\{text\\}}''.\nWe generate up to depth~4 to empirically confirm convergence: 5-class analysis shows adjacent depths 2--4 become indistinguishable (F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.27; Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}), motivating the gen-2+ class collapse.\nAll generation uses temperature $\\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.\nAfter filtering chain breaks and passages too short for feature extraction, 37{,}058 texts remain across all depths (Appendix~\\ref{app:dataset-stats}); depths 2--4 are collapsed into $2{+}$.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Extraction}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\n\\paragraph{Terminology.} Throughout this paper, we refer to per-token cross-entropy loss under a fixed reference model as ``entropy'' for conciseness; these are reference-model NLL values, not true information-theoretic entropy of the generating distribution.\n\nWe deliberately use interpretable distributional statistics so that the DDR partition remains transparent; end-to-end neural features improve classification ($+$11.6\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}) but obscure the decomposition.\nLeave-one-feature-out analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}) confirms that both reference-model and surface feature groups contribute independently.\n\nWe extract seven distributional features per text (Table~\\ref{tab:features}), selected to provide principled coverage of the NLL distribution---location (mean), spread (std), extremes (max, min), and tail structure (decay)---complemented by two reference-free surface diversity measures.\nWe evaluated six additional NLL statistics (median, 90th percentile, IQR, CV, skewness, kurtosis) as candidate features; all exhibit $|r| > 0.65$ with existing features and yield no classification gain when added ($\\Delta$F1\\,$\\leq$\\,$-0.011$; Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-candidate}).\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-model features.}\nA held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nAlthough Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the eight generators, cross-family reference validation (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}) shows F1 spread of only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references, confirming DDR is reference-model agnostic; however, all tested references are causal LMs.\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics: mean, standard deviation, maximum, and minimum of the token-level NLL, plus tail\\_decay---the slope of the vocabulary-level averaged log-probability distribution in ranks 100--1000, capturing the power-law regime where depth-induced compression is most detectable while avoiding high-frequency head noise and sparse extreme-tail counts.\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nTwo features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model: type-token ratio (TTR) and the proportion of words appearing exactly once (hapax\\_ratio).\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThese features exhibit a \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR), separating into drifting and non-drifting groups (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}; pairwise correlations in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}).\nTTR and hapax ratio are strongly correlated ($r = 0.97$); both are retained for marginal LOFO gains.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-validation.}\nWe evaluate generalization via \\textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: each fold holds out one generator entirely, so the classifier must generalize to a generator absent from training---the strictest per-model protocol for assessing deployment against previously unseen generators.\nLOGO does not isolate family-level effects; \\textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} addresses this by holding out entire model families (binary F1\\,=\\,0.686; \\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}).\nGenerator subsets vary across experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:gen-subsets-section}).\n\n\\paragraph{Classifier.}\nWe use XGBoost \\cite{xgboost} throughout ($n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}6$, learning rate $0.1$; fixed without tuning), with identical hyperparameters across all methods in Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}.\nAlternative classifiers (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, CORN \\cite{corn_ordinal}, Frank-Hall \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}); discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, with 95\\% bootstrap CIs ($n{=}1{,}000$).\nAll $p$-values are two-sided with Bonferroni correction within each family of simultaneous tests.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation to ensure a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify depth signal is not a length artifact via length-matched subsampling that equalizes length distributions across depth classes (\\S\\ref{sec:results-classification}; Appendix~\\ref{app:length}).\n",

    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. All three figures are in results.tex (Section 4, which starts on page 5-6). The user wants a visual figure in the first 4 pages. 

The best approach for empirical papers is to have a "teaser figure" early in the paper - typically in the Introduction or right after. Figure 1 (DDR trajectory, full-width figure*) shows the key finding and would work well as a teaser in the Introduction.

However, moving a `figure*` from results.tex to introduction.tex might cause LaTeX float placement issues. The `figure*` environment in two-column layouts can only appear at the top of a page, and LaTeX defers it to the next page if there's not enough room.


I'm weighing the tradeoffs: moving Figure 1 to the Introduction would be most impactful since it's the key finding, but as a full-width figure it would likely land on page 2 anyway. Figure 2 would be easier to place since it's single-column, or I could try forcing earlier placement with aggressive positioning hints while keeping it in results.tex. ```text
50	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
51	\label{sec:results-differential}
52	
53	\begin{figure*}[t]
54	\centering
55	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}
56	\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth ($y$-axis: $z$-scored feature values). \textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\rho| > 0.59$). \textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (blue for drifting, amber for stationary). This dichotomy defines the DDR.}
57	\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}
58	\end{figure*}
59	
60	\paragraph{Formal definition.}
61	For feature $f$ measured across depths $d \in \{0,1,2{+}\}$, we define its \emph{depth sensitivity} as $\text{DDR}(f) = |\rho(\text{depth}, f)|$, where $\rho$ is Spearman correlation over all per-text measurements.
62	A feature is classified as \emph{drifting} if Cohen's $d > 0.50$ between adjacent depths, or \emph{stationary} if $d < 0.15$; these thresholds are reference anchors, not definitive boundaries---the two-cluster structure is robust across $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$, supported by unsupervised $k$-means (silhouette\,=\,0.660, $p{=}0.030$), exhaustive enumeration of all 35 splits (this partition ranks first), and independent replication in all eight generators (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}); crucially, each generator's data is fully independent, making per-generator split analysis an 8-fold independent validation of the partition rather than same-data circularity.
63	Under autoregressive generation, recursive inputs grow increasingly self-typical---we hypothesize this reflects the mixing property of finite-vocabulary autoregressive processes~\citep{markovian_chains}---predicting that location statistics should drift while shape statistics remain invariant (confirmed across nucleus, greedy, and top-$k$ strategies; Appendix~\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).
64	Shape features (extreme ranges, tail decay) reflect architectural constraints rather than input location, explaining their stability under recursive compression.
65	
66	The classification results above establish \emph{that} depth is distinguishable; we now ask \emph{why} by examining which features respond to recursive generation.
67	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} reveals a consistent dichotomy across all eight generators: central-tendency features (entropy mean (reference-model NLL; \S\ref{sec:features}), TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat.
68	Appendix~\ref{app:qualitative} illustrates the DDR with a concrete generation chain.
69	The primary partition is binary: $k{=}2$ clustering cleanly separates three drifting from four non-drifting features; higher $k$ degrades quality ($k{=}3$: silhouette\,=\,0.532; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
70	A domain-informed refinement resolves entropy std as a sole \emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.55$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; CV shift $<$3\% vs.\ $>$15\% for drifting features).
71	Multiple effect-size measures corroborate this partition (Quantification below).
72	
73	\begin{figure}[t!]
74	\centering
75	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
76	\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values. Drifting features (blue; $|\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) show strong monotonic depth sensitivity; non-drifting features remain stable. Entropy std (wine; $d{=}0.55$) is an intermediate member; near-stationary features (amber; $d \leq 0.10$) show negligible drift. Entropy min ($|\rho|{=}0.40$ pooled; gen-0/gen-1 sensitive) and tail decay (TOST 4/8) are boundary features within this group. This location/shape asymmetry---the DDR---enables depth classification without per-generator calibration. Marker shapes encode feature identity for grayscale accessibility.}
77	\label{fig:feature-drift}
78	\end{figure}
79	
80	Pooled Spearman correlations with depth corroborate the binary split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
81	drifting features show substantial monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.64$, TTR $\rho = -0.63$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.60$),
82	while non-drifting features remain near-zero (tail decay $|\rho| = 0.04$; per-depth distributions in Appendix~\ref{app:feature-distributions}).
83	The six-fold magnitude gap between drifting ($|\rho| > 0.60$) and non-drifting features ($|\rho| \leq 0.16$) defines the DDR partition.
84	Within the non-drifting group, entropy std ($|\rho| = 0.16$, $d{=}0.55$) shows a substantial level shift despite low monotonicity, distinguishing it from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$); this refinement is stable across $d$ thresholds in $[0.56, 0.75]$ (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
85	
86	\paragraph{Quantification.}
87	Parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures converge on the split.
88	Cohen's $d$ \cite{cohen1988statistical} shows a ${\geq}7\times$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and near-stationary features ($d \leq 0.08$); KS statistics, Glass's $\Delta$, Cliff's $\delta$, and CV shift all corroborate (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}), with the partition robust under heteroscedasticity (Glass's $\Delta$ gap ${\geq}9.5\times$; Appendix~\ref{app:heteroscedasticity}).
89	Four features fall cleanly into drifting or non-drifting groups.
90	Entropy min and tail decay are \emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1 ($|\rho|{=}0.40$); tail decay is \emph{weakly stationary}, passing TOST for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).
91	Entropy std ($d{=}0.55$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$) is the sole intermediate member.
92	\paragraph{Validation.}
93	Per-generator split analysis provides the strongest validation: each of the eight hold-out generators independently recovers the identical drifting/non-drifting partition (per-generator silhouette range 0.505--0.760, invariant across $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
94	TOST equivalence testing confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8 pass) and entropy max (6/8); tail decay passes for only 4/8 ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$), reflecting generator-specific tail structure variation rather than systematic drift: the effect sizes remain an order of magnitude below the drifting group ($d \geq 0.76$), and the partition assignment is unchanged.
95	Permutation testing confirms the split as non-random ($p{=}0.030$); reclassifying either boundary feature preserves the core split.
96	
97	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
98	The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.773, 10-fold stratified CV, complementing the LOGO protocol in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table}).
99	The collinear drifting triplet captures ${\sim}$1 effective dimension (PCA PC1\,=\,92.3\%; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}); group ablation confirms complementarity for depth profiling: 3-class F1 gains $+$5.3\,pp when non-drifting features are added (drifting-only 0.716, all-7 0.769), while binary detection is already saturated by location drift alone (drifting-only binary F1\,=\,0.697 vs.\ all-7\,=\,0.700; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table})---shape stability contributes to depth \emph{grading}, not detection.
100	TTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are retained for marginal LOFO gains \cite{strobl_conditional_2008} (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
101	
102	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
103	To isolate reference-model bias, we evaluate on a 5-generator subset excluding all Pythia-family generators---a stricter protocol than 8-gen LOGO.
104	Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable performance (F1 spread\,=\,0.013; Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref} in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}), with drift direction consistently negative.
105	Same-family proximity may inflate Pythia F1 by up to 1.3\,pp, but the DDR partition is fully preserved regardless of reference choice.
106	
107	\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}
108	All four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-$9.3 to $-$26.0\,pp binary F1; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); continuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\,=\,0.781; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
109	The DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).
110	
111	
112	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
113	\label{sec:results-baselines}
114	
115	We next ask whether existing detection methods---designed for binary real-vs-synthetic classification---incidentally capture depth structure, repurposing four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
116	The strongest baseline is a 4-detector + surface feature ensemble (binary F1\,=\,0.708), which pools four detection scores with TTR and hapax ratio; among individual detectors, Avg-LogProb provides the most meaningful single-feature comparison.
117	
118	\begin{table}[t]
119	\centering
120	\footnotesize
121	\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled evaluation; pooled F1 is slightly higher than per-generator macro F1 in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results} because larger test folds from high-performing generators contribute proportionally more). Identical XGBoost configuration. 7-feature: one 410M model (905\,MB); 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B params (2{,}868\,MB). Individual detection scores (N/A) share the reference-model forward pass; per-method timing is not separately profiled.}
122	\label{tab:baselines}
123	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
124	\toprule
125	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{Bin F1} & \textbf{ms/text} \\
126	\midrule
127	4-det.\ + surface     & 6 & \textbf{0.795} & \textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\
128	4-detector ens.\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\
129	7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \textbf{9.7} \\
130	\midrule
131	Avg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\
132	FastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\
133	LD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\
134	Binoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\

142	\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\ inference cost in ms/text (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble (one 410M reference model) matches multi-detector ensembles (two models, 1.4B total) at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost; individual detectors vary widely when repurposed for depth. Inference costs measured on a single NVIDIA RTX 5090.}
143	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
144	\end{figure}
145	
146	The 7-feature ensemble matches the 4-detector+surface ensemble (binary F1: 0.705 vs.\ 0.708; Figure~\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost, surpassing all individual detectors by 3.0--16.6\,pp.
147	DDR's contribution is the decomposition into location-sensitive and shape-stable components (\S\ref{sec:discussion}), with classification performance comparable to baseline ensembles rather than a performance advance.
148	Ordinal-specific methods (CORAL, Frank-Hall) yield comparable F1 under this setup (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}), suggesting limited advantage from ranking-aware losses at 3-class resolution.

higher $k$ degrades quality ($k{=}3$: silhouette\\,=\\,0.532; Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\nA domain-informed refinement resolves entropy std as a sole \\emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.55$, $|\\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; CV shift $<$3\\% vs.\\ $>$15\\% for drifting features).\nMultiple effect-size measures corroborate this partition (Quantification below).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t!]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values. Drifting features (blue; $|\\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) show strong monotonic depth sensitivity; non-drifting features remain stable. Entropy std (wine; $d{=}0.55$) is an intermediate member; near-stationary features (amber; $d \\leq 0.10$) show negligible drift. Entropy min ($|\\rho|{=}0.40$ pooled; gen-0/gen-1 sensitive) and tail decay (TOST 4/8) are boundary features within this group. This location/shape asymmetry---the DDR---enables depth classification without per-generator calibration. Marker shapes encode feature identity for grayscale accessibility.}\n\\label{fig:feature-drift}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPooled Spearman correlations with depth corroborate the binary split (Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}):\ndrifting features show substantial monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\\rho = -0.64$, TTR $\\rho = -0.63$, entropy mean $\\rho = -0.60$),\nwhile non-drifting features remain near-zero (tail decay $|\\rho| = 0.04$; per-depth distributions in Appendix~\\ref{app:feature-distributions}).\nThe six-fold magnitude gap between drifting ($|\\rho| > 0.60$) and non-drifting features ($|\\rho| \\leq 0.16$) defines the DDR partition.\nWithin the non-drifting group, entropy std ($|\\rho| = 0.16$, $d{=}0.55$) shows a substantial level shift despite low monotonicity, distinguishing it from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$); this refinement is stable across $d$ thresholds in $[0.56, 0.75]$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Quantification.}\nParametric and non-parametric effect-size measures converge on the split.\nCohen's $d$ \\cite{cohen1988statistical} shows a ${\\geq}7\\times$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and near-stationary features ($d \\leq 0.08$); KS statistics, Glass's $\\Delta$, Cliff's $\\delta$, and CV shift all corroborate (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}), with the partition robust under heteroscedasticity (Glass's $\\Delta$ gap ${\\geq}9.5\\times$; Appendix~\\ref{app:heteroscedasticity}).\nFour features fall cleanly into drifting or non-drifting groups.\nEntropy min and tail decay are \\emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1 ($|\\rho|{=}0.40$); tail decay is \\emph{weakly stationary}, passing TOST for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).\nEntropy std ($d{=}0.55$, $|\\rho|{=}0.16$) is the sole intermediate member.\n\\paragraph{Validation.}\nPer-generator split analysis provides the strongest validation: each of the eight hold-out generators independently recovers the identical drifting/non-drifting partition (per-generator silhouette range 0.505--0.760, invariant across $\\varepsilon \\in [0.10, 0.75]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\nTOST equivalence testing confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8 pass) and entropy max (6/8); tail decay passes for only 4/8 ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$), reflecting generator-specific tail structure variation rather than systematic drift: the effect sizes remain an order of magnitude below the drifting group ($d \\geq 0.76$), and the partition assignment is unchanged.\nPermutation testing confirms the split as non-random ($p{=}0.030$); reclassifying either boundary feature preserves the core split.\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThe full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.773, 10-fold stratified CV, complementing the LOGO protocol in Table~\\ref{tab:logo-results}) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \\approx 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation-table}).\nThe collinear drifting triplet captures ${\\sim}$1 effective dimension (PCA PC1\\,=\\,92.3\\%; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}); group ablation confirms complementarity for depth profiling: 3-class F1 gains $+$5.3\\,pp when non-drifting features are added (drifting-only 0.716, all-7 0.769), while binary detection is already saturated by location drift alone (drifting-only binary F1\\,=\\,0.697 vs.\\ all-7\\,=\\,0.700; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation-table})---shape stability contributes to depth \\emph{grading}, not detection.\nTTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are retained for marginal LOFO gains \\cite{strobl_conditional_2008} (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}\nTo isolate reference-model bias, we evaluate on a 5-generator subset excluding all Pythia-family generators---a stricter protocol than 8-gen LOGO.\nAlternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable performance (F1 spread\\,=\\,0.013; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-ref} in Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}), with drift direction consistently negative.\nSame-family proximity may inflate Pythia F1 by up to 1.3\\,pp, but the DDR partition is fully preserved regardless of reference choice.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}\nAll four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-$9.3 to $-$26.0\\,pp binary F1; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); continuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\\,=\\,0.781; Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).\nThe DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}\n\\label{sec:results-baselines}\n\nWe next ask whether existing detection methods---designed for binary real-vs-synthetic classification---incidentally capture depth structure, repurposing four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\nThe strongest baseline is a 4-detector + surface feature ensemble (binary F1\\,=\\,0.708), which pools four detection scores with TTR and hapax ratio; among individual detectors, Avg-LogProb provides the most meaningful single-feature comparison.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled evaluation; pooled F1 is slightly higher than per-generator macro F1 in Table~\\ref{tab:logo-results} because larger test folds from high-performing generators contribute proportionally more). Identical XGBoost configuration. 7-feature: one 410M model (905\\,MB); 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B params (2{,}868\\,MB). Individual detection scores (N/A) share the reference-model forward pass; per-method timing is not separately profiled.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Feat.} & \\textbf{3-class F1} & \\textbf{Bin F1} & \\textbf{ms/text} \\\\\n\\midrule\n4-det.\\ + surface     & 6 & \\textbf{0.795} & \\textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\\\\n4-detector ens.\\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\\\\n7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \\textbf{9.7} \\\\\n\\midrule\nAvg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\\\\nFastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\\\\nLD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\\\\nBinoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\\ inference cost in ms/text (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble (one 410M reference model) matches multi-detector ensembles (two models, 1.4B total) at ${\\sim}2.4\\times$ lower cost; individual detectors vary widely when repurposed for depth. Inference costs measured on a single NVIDIA RTX 5090.}\n\\label{fig:baseline-ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe 7-feature ensemble matches the 4-detector+surface ensemble (binary F1: 0.705 vs.\\ 0.708; Figure~\\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) at ${\\sim}2.4\\times$ lower cost, surpassing all individual detectors by 3.0--16.6\\,pp.\nDDR's contribution is the decomposition into location-sensitive and shape-stable components (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}), with classification performance comparable to baseline ensembles rather than a performance advance.\nOrdinal-specific methods (CORAL, Frank-Hall) yield comparable F1 under this setup (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}), suggesting limited advantage from ranking-aware losses at 3-class resolution.\n",

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	An estimated 7--17\% of recent scientific publications already contain substantial LLM-modified content \cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}. As these outputs re-enter training corpora \cite{dolma,fineweb} and seed further generation, text accumulates at varying \emph{generation depths}; each successive cycle reshapes distributional properties in ways that matter for data provenance, corpus auditing, and contamination monitoring in base-model pretraining corpora \cite{ccnet,dsir} as well as model collapse analysis \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}. Understanding \emph{how} recursive generation alters text distributions---and which properties change versus which are preserved---is therefore a prerequisite for principled corpus curation, yet current methods address only binary human-or-machine detection \cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score}.
5	Base models provide the cleanest substrate to isolate depth effects from alignment-induced changes.
6	We find that recursive generation produces a decomposable depth profile: distributional \emph{location} drifts with depth while \emph{shape} remains stable across generators, an asymmetry sufficient for cross-generator depth prediction.
7	
8	Distributional compression under recursive generation is well-documented \cite{curious_decline,broken_telephone,markovian_chains}, yet existing analyses characterize \emph{that} and \emph{why} convergence occurs without decomposing what drifts from what is preserved.
9	We introduce \emph{depth profiling}, classifying text by ordinal generation depth (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+), and decompose this compression into structurally distinct components: across eight base-model generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B), the NLL distribution's central tendency (mean entropy (reference-model NLL), lexical diversity) drifts monotonically with depth while its shape (entropy extremes, tail structure) remains an order of magnitude more stable ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$ vs.\ $d < 0.10$ for three near-stationary features; entropy std is intermediate ($d{=}0.55$); boundary features show additional nuance; convergent effect-size measures confirm this partition).
10	We term this location/shape asymmetry the \emph{differential depth response} (DDR; Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}), an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation that is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion}, which erodes tail coverage via weight updates; DDR instead analyzes fixed-parameter inference chains.
11	The stationary shape component provides the cross-generator reference needed for depth prediction without per-generator calibration.
12	We focus on base (pretrained) models; instruction tuning degrades depth signal (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}).
13	
14	As a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\,=\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at ${\sim}2.4\times$ lower cost; an end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 (6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.
15	
16	Our contributions:
17	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
18	    \item \textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Across eight generators from five families, we decompose inference-chain drift into a location component that shifts monotonically ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and a shape component that remains near-stationary ($d < 0.10$ for three near-stationary features). Where prior work documents \emph{what converges} \cite{curious_decline,markovian_chains}, DDR identifies \emph{what is approximately preserved} and enables depth detection without per-generator calibration. DDR is a descriptive empirical finding; the classification value lies in this decomposition rather than the classifier itself.
19	    \item \textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts from eight generators across five families (0.6B--8B), with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation as the primary evaluation protocol.
20	    \item \textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, and model scale maps the operational envelope of depth profiling, identifying key challenges in instruction-tuned models, cross-domain transfer, and perturbation robustness.
21	\end{enumerate}
22

    "oldString": "We term this location/shape asymmetry the \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR; Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}), an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation that is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion}, which erodes tail coverage via weight updates; DDR instead analyzes fixed-parameter inference chains.\nThe stationary shape component provides the cross-generator reference needed for depth prediction without per-generator calibration.",
    "newString": "We term this location/shape asymmetry the \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR; Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}), an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation that is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion}, which erodes tail coverage via weight updates; DDR instead analyzes fixed-parameter inference chains.\nThe stationary shape component provides the cross-generator reference needed for depth prediction without per-generator calibration.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t!]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Differential depth response (DDR).     "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nAn estimated 7--17\\% of recent scientific publications already contain substantial LLM-modified content \\cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}. As these outputs re-enter training corpora \\cite{dolma,fineweb} and seed further generation, text accumulates at varying \\emph{generation depths}; each successive cycle reshapes distributional properties in ways that matter for data provenance, corpus auditing, and contamination monitoring in base-model pretraining corpora \\cite{ccnet,dsir} as well as model collapse analysis \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}. Understanding \\emph{how} recursive generation alters text distributions---and which properties change versus which are preserved---is therefore a prerequisite for principled corpus curation, yet current methods address only binary human-or-machine detection \\cite{detectgpt,fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score}.\nBase models provide the cleanest substrate to isolate depth effects from alignment-induced changes.\nWe find that recursive generation produces a decomposable depth profile: distributional \\emph{location} drifts with depth while \\emph{shape} remains stable across generators, an asymmetry sufficient for cross-generator depth prediction.\n\nDistributional compression under recursive generation is well-documented \\cite{curious_decline,broken_telephone,markovian_chains}, yet existing analyses characterize \\emph{that} and \\emph{why} convergence occurs without decomposing what drifts from what is preserved.\nWe introduce \\emph{depth profiling}, classifying text by ordinal generation depth (gen-0\\,/\\,gen-1\\,/\\,gen-2+), and decompose this compression into structurally distinct components: across eight base-model generators spanning five architectural families (0.6B--8B), the NLL distribution's central tendency (mean entropy (reference-model NLL), lexical diversity) drifts monotonically with depth while its shape (entropy extremes, tail structure) remains an order of magnitude more stable ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$ vs.\\ $d < 0.10$ for three near-stationary features; entropy std is intermediate ($d{=}0.55$); boundary features show additional nuance; convergent effect-size measures confirm this partition).\nWe term this location/shape asymmetry the \\emph{differential depth response} (DDR; Figure~\\ref{fig:feature-drift}), an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation that is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion}, which erodes tail coverage via weight updates; DDR instead analyzes fixed-parameter inference chains.\nThe stationary shape component provides the cross-generator reference needed for depth prediction without per-generator calibration.\nWe focus on base (pretrained) models; instruction tuning degrades depth signal (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}).\n\nAs a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at ${\\sim}2.4\\times$ lower cost; an end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.905 (6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features.\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{Empirical decomposition (DDR).} Across eight generators from five families, we decompose inference-chain drift into a location component that shifts monotonically ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and a shape component that remains near-stationary ($d < 0.10$ for three near-stationary features). Where prior work documents \\emph{what converges} \\cite{curious_decline,markovian_chains}, DDR identifies \\emph{what is approximately preserved} and enables depth detection without per-generator calibration. DDR is a descriptive empirical finding; the classification value lies in this decomposition rather than the classifier itself.\n    \\item \\textbf{Task formulation and evaluation suite.} We define generation depth classification as an ordinal prediction task and construct a controlled dataset of 37K texts from eight generators across five families (0.6B--8B), with leave-one-generator-out cross-validation as the primary evaluation protocol.\n    \\item \\textbf{Scope characterization.} Systematic evaluation across temperature, prompt type, instruction tuning, and model scale maps the operational envelope of depth profiling, identifying key challenges in instruction-tuned models, cross-domain transfer, and perturbation robustness.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

          " As a proof of concept, a lightweight 7-feature ensemble informed by DDR achieves leak-free binary F1\\,=\\,0.694 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, matching multi-detector ensembles at ${\\sim}2.4\\times$ lower cost; an end-to-end QLoRA classifier reaches 3-class F1\\,=\\,0.905 (6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that the depth signal extends well beyond handcrafted features."
        ]
      }
    ],

I need to trim the Discussion section to pull everything back within the page limit. Let me look at what's in the Discussion to find compression opportunities.

I'm also noticing whitespace issues on pages 25 and 36 that could be tightened up. ```text
1	\vspace{-2mm}
2	\section{Discussion}
3	\label{sec:discussion}
4	\vspace{-2mm}
5	
6	%% Mechanism — why DDR exists
7	\looseness=-1 We hypothesize that DDR reflects an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation: recursive inputs compress the token-level NLL distribution monotonically (Appendix~\ref{app:nll-distribution}), producing location drift independent of the token-selection mechanism.
8	Controlled ablations support this: greedy decoding preserves the full partition (Appendix~\ref{app:decoding-strategy}), temperature modulates drift magnitude while preserving the partition (Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), and semantic-constraint attenuation confirms predictions (Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
9	ANOVA quantifies the asymmetry: $\eta^2_{\text{depth}}{=}0.34$--$0.37$ for location vs.\ ${<}0.01$ for shape (Appendix~\ref{app:variance-decomposition}).
10	
11	%% Implications — what DDR enables
12	\looseness=-1 Depth constitutes a new analytical dimension for corpus auditing; although per-instance gen-2+ recall is limited (${\sim}$27\%), batch contamination estimation reaches ${\pm}3$--$4$\% at $n{=}1{,}000$ (Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}), sufficient for auditing where the goal is contamination proportion rather than per-text judgment.
13	
14	%% Scope \& Connections — where DDR works and fails
15	\looseness=-1 DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location; DDR complements \citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics; where the latter bounds distinguishable depth, DDR adds location/shape decomposition enabling cross-generator classification without per-generator calibration---all three observe saturation at ${\sim}$3--5 iterations (Appendix~\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).
16	Instruction tuning compresses DDR (6/7 features show $\rho$ reversal), though QLoRA recovers F1\,=\,0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}); cross-domain signal persists at F1\,=\,0.74--0.75 (Appendix~\ref{app:domain}) and degrades gracefully under token replacement ($-$4.6\,pp at 5\%; Appendix~\ref{app:human-edit}).
17	\vspace{-1mm}
18	
```

    "newString": "\\looseness=-1 DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}---retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location---and complements \\citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \\citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics by adding location/shape decomposition; all three observe saturation at ${\\sim}$3--5 iterations (Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).\nInstruction tuning compresses DDR, though QLoRA recovers F1\\,=\\,0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}); cross-domain signal persists at F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75 (Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}).\n\\vspace{-2mm}",
    "originalFile": "\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\n%% Mechanism — why DDR exists\n\\looseness=-1 We hypothesize that DDR reflects an empirical regularity of base-model autoregressive generation: recursive inputs compress the token-level NLL distribution monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}), producing location drift independent of the token-selection mechanism.\nControlled ablations support this: greedy decoding preserves the full partition (Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}), temperature modulates drift magnitude while preserving the partition (Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), and semantic-constraint attenuation confirms predictions (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).\nANOVA quantifies the asymmetry: $\\eta^2_{\\text{depth}}{=}0.34$--$0.37$ for location vs.\\ ${<}0.01$ for shape (Appendix~\\ref{app:variance-decomposition}).\n\n%% Implications — what DDR enables\n\\looseness=-1 Depth constitutes a new analytical dimension for corpus auditing; although per-instance gen-2+ recall is limited (${\\sim}$27\\%), batch contamination estimation reaches ${\\pm}3$--$4$\\% at $n{=}1{,}000$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}), sufficient for auditing where the goal is contamination proportion rather than per-text judgment.\n\n%% Scope \\& Connections — where DDR works and fails\n\\looseness=-1 DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage via weight updates, whereas inference-chain drift shifts only location; DDR complements \\citeauthor{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation and \\citeauthor{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics; where the latter bounds distinguishable depth, DDR adds location/shape decomposition enabling cross-generator classification without per-generator calibration---all three observe saturation at ${\\sim}$3--5 iterations (Appendix~\\ref{app:iterative-comparison}).\nInstruction tuning compresses DDR (6/7 features show $\\rho$ reversal), though QLoRA recovers F1\\,=\\,0.89--0.91 (Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}); cross-domain signal persists at F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75 (Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}) and degrades gracefully under token replacement ($-$4.6\\,pp at 5\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:human-edit}).\n\\vspace{-1mm}\n",

```text
1	\begingroup
2	\setlength{\parskip}{2pt plus 1pt}
3	
4	\subsection*{DDR Trend Lines}
5	
6	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trends} shows the per-feature depth trend (mean across all samples at each depth) with individual generator traces overlaid.
7	The consistent downward trajectories for drifting features across all generators confirm that DDR is a universal property of recursive generation.
8	Shape features exhibit flat trajectories regardless of generator, with near-zero $R^2$ for the linear fit.
9	$R^2$ values are computed from ordinary least-squares regression on depth-level means (five points per generator, one per depth 0--4), not per-sample regression; the annotated Spearman $\rho$ provides the complementary per-sample monotonicity measure.
10	
11	\begin{figure}[H]
12	\centering
13	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_appendix_ddr_trends.pdf}
14	\caption{Per-feature depth trends. Bold line: pooled mean ($\pm$1.96\,SE shading) across all generators, colored by DDR zone (blue: drifting, wine: intermediate, amber: near-stationary). Thin traces: individual generators. Spearman $\rho$ and linear $R^2$ are annotated per panel.}
15	\label{fig:ddr-trends}
16	\end{figure}
17	
18	\subsection*{UMAP Embedding}
19	
20	Figure~\ref{fig:umap} shows a 2D UMAP projection of the 7-feature space (7{,}500 stratified samples, StandardScaler normalization, $k$\,=\,30 neighbors, min\_dist\,=\,0.3).
21	Panel~(a) reveals a depth gradient from gen-0 through gen-4, with progressive overlap at deeper generations consistent with the convergence that motivates the gen-2+ class collapse.
22	Panel~(b) shows the 3-class view: gen-0 forms a separable cluster, while gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap substantially, explaining the classification difficulty at this boundary.
23	
24	\endgroup
25	
26	\begin{figure}[H]
27	\centering
28	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth, trim=0 0 356pt 0, clip]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\\[-2pt]
29	{\small (a) Colored by raw generation depth (0--4)}\\[6pt]
30	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth, trim=356pt 0 0 0, clip]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\\[-2pt]
31	{\small (b) Colored by 3-class label}
32	\caption{UMAP 2D projection of the 7-feature space ($N$\,=\,7{,}500 stratified sample). (a) A gradient structure emerges, with deeper depths converging. (b) Gen-0 separates clearly; gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap, consistent with the high false-negative rate at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.}
33	\label{fig:umap}
34	\end{figure}
35

Spearman $\\rho$ and linear $R^2$ are annotated per panel.}\n\\label{fig:ddr-trends}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection*{UMAP Embedding}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:umap} shows a 2D UMAP projection of the 7-feature space (7{,}500 stratified samples, StandardScaler normalization, $k$\\,=\\,30 neighbors, min\\_dist\\,=\\,0.3).\nPanel~(a) reveals a depth gradient from gen-0 through gen-4, with progressive overlap at deeper generations consistent with the convergence that motivates the gen-2+ class collapse.\nPanel~(b) shows the 3-class view: gen-0 forms a separable cluster, while gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap substantially, explaining the classification difficulty at this boundary.\n\n\\endgroup\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=0 0 356pt 0, clip]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (a) Colored by raw generation depth (0--4)}\\\\[6pt]\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth, trim=356pt 0 0 0, clip]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}\\\\[-2pt]\n{\\small (b) Colored by 3-class label}\n\\caption{UMAP 2D projection of the 7-feature space ($N$\\,=\\,7{,}500 stratified sample). (a) A gradient structure emerges, with deeper depths converging. (b) Gen-0 separates clearly; gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap, consistent with the high false-negative rate at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.}\n\\label{fig:umap}\n\\end{figure}\n",
      "numLines": 35,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 35
    }
  },

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KJLiQ361R4zZhbj31DTynu`</summary>

```text
40	This is not a data-quality artifact: the Gemma-2-2B partition contains 4{,}957 instances with a balanced depth distribution ($\sim$990 per level).
41	A plausible explanation is that Gemma-2's first-generation continuations occupy a region of feature space highly similar to human-written text (gen-0), causing systematic misclassification of gen-1 as gen-0 or gen-2+.
42	This constitutes an architecture-specific generalization limitation rather than a systemic failure of the depth profiling approach.
43	Several architectural differences may explain this behavior: (a)~Gemma-2's distinct attention mechanism and SentencePiece tokenizer with a larger vocabulary may produce first-generation continuations whose feature-space signature closely resembles human text, causing systematic gen-0/gen-1 confusion; (b)~this anomaly does not affect DDR's validity for the remaining eight generators, where per-generator F1 shifts by at most 0.011 upon Gemma inclusion.
44	
45	\paragraph{Scale effect.}
46	A clear parameter-count gradient emerges across the nine generators: three of four sub-1.7B generators achieve F1\,$>$\,0.81 (OLMo-2-1B at 0.773 is the exception), mid-range models (2--3B) cluster at 0.63--0.81, and large models (7B+) fall to 0.60--0.70.
47	Qwen3-8B (8.2B parameters) achieves 3-class F1\,=\,0.696 under the 9-gen split, compared to 0.701 in the class-balanced 8-gen evaluation; this small difference reflects the changed training composition rather than a property of the Qwen3-8B generator itself.
48	
49	\paragraph{14B-parameter evidence.}
50	Preliminary evidence from a 14.7B generator (Qwen3-14B-Base) confirms that the DDR partition persists at larger scale: Cohen's $d$ for drifting features remains in the large-effect range ($d{=}0.78$--$1.12$), though Spearman $|\rho|$ attenuates by 21--35\%, with entropy mean falling just below the drifting threshold ($|\rho|{=}0.39 < 0.40$, $d{=}0.78$).
51	This scale-dependent signal compression is consistent with larger models producing more self-typical outputs that narrow inter-depth contrasts; the partition structure is preserved even as absolute effect sizes compress.
52	
```

Now I understand the page 25 issue. The `appendix_feature_distributions.tex` has two figures:
1. DDR Trend Lines figure (line 11-16) - using `\begin{figure}[H]` 
2. UMAP Embedding figure (line 26-34) - using `\begin{figure}[H]`

The `[H]` placement forces the figures to be placed exactly where they are in the text. If the figures don't fill the page, there's whitespace. The issue is that the two figures are placed with text in between, and LaTeX can't move them to fill space optimally.


Looking at page 25, the DDR Trend Lines and UMAP figures are stacked with whitespace between them, and the UMAP figure itself has two panels arranged vertically. I could address this by enlarging the figures to consume more space, or by pulling text from the next page up through tighter spacing and figure adjustments. For page 36, I need to examine what comes after the 14B-parameter section and the iterative comparison to see where the whitespace is occurring.

The left column of page 36 ends with the 14B-parameter evidence text and then has blank space before the "Comparison with Concurrent Iterative-Generation Studies" heading appears in the right column. The gap might be caused by Table 48 being placed on a different page. Before diving into these figure adjustments, I should prioritize compressing the Discussion section to meet the 8-page limit, then tackle the appendix whitespace issues.

Looking at page 8: The Conclusion section "6 Conclusion" now fits entirely on page 8! The right column ends with "extending to instruct models remains open." and there's a bit of space below it. 

Page 9 starts with "Limitations" — which is outside the 8-page main text limit (EMNLP allows unlimited space for Limitations and Ethics). So the 8-page constraint is now met.

The Discussion compression worked. Now I need to handle the appendix whitespace issues on pages 25 and 36.

For page 25, the issue is the Feature Distributions section with the DDR Trend Lines figure and UMAP figure having whitespace. 

I'm looking at the appendix_feature_distributions.tex file and noticing the figures are set with `[H]` placement, which locks them in place and can create gaps. I could try relaxing this to `[t]` or `[ht]` to let LaTeX position them more flexibly and fill the whitespace better.

Looking more closely at page 25, the DDR Trend Lines text sits at the top with a large blank area below it, then Figure 8 appears in the bottom left and the UMAP embedding in the bottom right—the figures seem quite small relative to the available space. Since this appears to be a two-column layout, the trend lines figure is constrained to `\columnwidth`, which is roughly half the page width, but there's still significant unused vertical space that could be tightened up. The left column has "DDR Trend Lines" text and then whitespace, then Figure 8 at the bottom. The right column has "UMAP Embedding" text at the top and Figure 9 text/images.

The issue is that Figure 8 uses `[H]` but the figure is placed after a few lines of text, and the remaining column space is blank above the figure. Looking at page 25, I see "Variance decomposition" text ending, then a "Feature Distributions" section header, then "DDR Trend Lines" with description text, followed by a large blank space before Figure 8 appears at the bottom. The `[H]` placement specifier should force the figure to stay in place, but it seems like a page break might be happening before the figure gets rendered. I'm thinking the simplest solution could be removing the `\begingroup`/`\endgroup` wrapper, adjusting spacing, or using `\raggedbottom` to prevent LaTeX from stretching content vertically. I should try removing the `\parskip` setting and `\begingroup/\endgroup` wrappers to let the text flow more naturally, or add more content to fill the gaps. Let me render pages 24-25 and 35-36 together to see the full context and understand what's causing these whitespace issues.

Now I can see the issue clearly:

**Page 24**: Full of content - tables and text fill both columns completely.

**Page 25**: 
- Top left: "Variance decomposition" text (the end of the previous section)
- Middle left: blank space, then a section bullet "Feature Distributions" and "DDR Trend Lines" subsection text
- Bottom half: mostly blank with Figure 8 (trend lines) in the center-right area and "UMAP Embedding" text on the right

The problem is that:
1. The "Variance decomposition" text finishes about 1/3 down the left column


2. Then "Feature Distributions" section starts with DDR Trend Lines text
3. Figure 8 (trend lines) is a `[H]` figure that appears small in the bottom center
4. The UMAP section and figure are in the right portion

There's a huge gap between the Variance Decomposition text ending and the Feature Distributions section starting. Also, the figures are quite small. The fix likely involves reducing the extra spacing that the `\begingroup` and `\parskip` settings are adding in the feature distributions file, enlarging the trend lines figure, and potentially using negative vertical spacing to pull content up.

Let me trace through the appendix includes in main.tex to understand the page layout and where this gap is coming from. I need to check what's triggering the large space between the variance decomposition conclusion and the feature distributions header, and whether there's an explicit page break or section formatting causing it. Looking at how the appendix sections are organized in the main file to understand the page layout and where these gaps might be coming from.

    "originalFile": "Table~\\ref{tab:iterative-comparison} compares this work with two concurrent studies on iterative LLM generation.\nAll three examine how repeated inference transforms text, but differ in chain operator, analytic lens, and downstream goal.\nOur continuation-chain setting permits richer depth signal and enables the location/shape decomposition that defines DDR.\n\n\\begin{table*}[!b]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Comparison with concurrent iterative-generation studies. All three examine how repeated LLM inference transforms text, but differ in chain operator, analytic lens, and downstream goal.}\n\\label{tab:iterative-comparison}\n\\resizebox{\\textwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{2.2cm} p{2.4cm} p{2.4cm} p{2.0cm} c p{3.4cm}@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Study} & \\textbf{Chain Type} & \\textbf{Feature Space} & \\textbf{Analysis Goal} & \\textbf{Generator Scale} & \\textbf{Steps} & \\textbf{Core Insight} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\citet{broken_telephone}\n  & Back-translation (6 bridge langs); rephrasing (ablation)\n  & Surface overlap (BLEU, ROUGE, METEOR, ChrF, BERTScore) + atomic factuality (FActScore)\n  & Factuality degradation rate across iterations\n  & 7--9B (3 open models)\n  & 100\n  & Factual accuracy decays progressively; rate governed by source--bridge linguistic distance \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\citet{markovian_chains}\n  & Rephrasing + round-trip translation\n  & String recurrence time, distinct-output count, similarity plateau (METEOR), KL contraction\n  & Convergence \\& diversity dynamics of inference-time Markov chains\n  & 7--8B (3 open + GPT-4o-mini)\n  & 50\n  & Greedy decoding collapses to 1--4 recurrent outputs; sampling sustains 10--20\\,+ realizations \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\textbf{Ours}\n  & Open-ended continuation (no meaning-preserving constraint)\n  & Token-level NLL statistics (location, scale, shape) + lexical diversity\n  & Depth classification + DDR decomposition into generator \\& depth effects\n  & 0.6--8B (8 models)\n  & 5\n  & NLL location drifts monotonically with depth while shape remains invariant---enabling per-generator depth profiling \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\paragraph{Finding-level connections.}\n\\citet{broken_telephone}'s factuality degradation under back-translation chains parallels DDR's location drift: both document monotonic loss of input-specific information with iteration depth, though the former measures semantic content loss while DDR captures distributional compression in the NLL feature space.\nTheir finding that degradation rate scales with source--bridge linguistic distance mirrors our observation that elaboration prompts attenuate DDR (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}): tighter semantic constraints slow both factuality decay and distributional drift.\n\n\\citet{markovian_chains}'s convergence dynamics under greedy decoding---collapse to 1--4 recurrent outputs within 50 steps---aligns with our greedy-decoding experiment where the DDR partition persists: shape features remain near-stationary (mean $|d|{=}0.05$) while location drift is maintained (mean $d{=}0.94$; Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}), confirming that DDR is an empirical regularity of autoregressive generation independent of sampling stochasticity.\nTheir KL contraction analysis complements DDR: where they quantify \\emph{how fast} the output distribution contracts, DDR decomposes \\emph{what} contracts (location) from what is preserved (shape).\n",
